Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n6\t\n7\tii. The index, and the session id built from an entry of it:\n8\t```python\n9\texp_info = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n10\tsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n11\t```\n12\t\n13\tThe three reads a session needs:\n14\t```python\n15\tbehavior = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\n16\tplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\n17\tarea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n18\t```\n19\t\n20\tiii. The index is reorganized from experiment type into subject and session, one record per recording, which is the form the conversion iterates over.\n21\t\n22\t## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n23\t\n24\ti. The mouse name is taken from `mname` in the index entry and carried on every record, and the records are grouped by it. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names, and `subject_idx` holds each session's index into that list: 89 sessions over 19 mice.\n25\t\n26\tii. The mouse of a record, and the subject list built from it:\n27\t```python\n28\trecords.append((entry['mname'], session_id, 'Beh_%s.npy' % exp_type, beh_key))\n29\t```\n30\t```python\n31\tsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n32\tsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n33\t```\n34\t\n35\tiii. The index already names the mouse, so no split has to be derived.\n36\t\n37\t## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n38\t\n39\ti. A session is one mouse on one date in one block, which the index entry gives as `mname`, `datexp` and `blk`. The id built from the three names the spike file and keys the behavior. A recording listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen, leaving 89 sessions.\n40\t\n41\tii. The id, and the check that keeps each recording once:\n42\t```python\n43\tsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n44\tif session_id in seen:\n45\t    continue\n46\tseen.add(session_id)\n47\t```\n48\t\n49\tiii. The recording sometimes repeat under different behavior session type. Therefore a recording that is listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen.\n50\t\n51\t## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n52\t\n53\ti. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n54\t\n55\tii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n56\t```python\n57\tframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n58\t```\n59\t\n60\tand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n61\t```python\n62\tinside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n63\treturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n64\t```\n65\t\n66\tiii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n67\t\n68\t## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n69\t\n70\ti. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n71\t\n72\tii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n73\t```python\n74\ttrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n75\t          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n76\t```\n77\t```python\n78\tlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\n79\treturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n80\t```\n81\t\n82\tiii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n83\t\n84\t## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n85\t\n86\ti. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n87\t\n88\tii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n89\t```python\n90\tplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\n91\tspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\n92\tarea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n93\t```\n94\t\n95\tiii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n96\t\n97\t## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n98\t\n99\ti. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n100\t\n101\tii. One trial of neural data:\n102\t```python\n103\tneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n104\t```\n105\t\n106\tiii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n107\t\n108\t## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n109\t\n110\ti. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n111\t\n112\tii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n113\t```python\n114\tregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\n115\tfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n116\t    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n117\t\n118\tkeep = region >= 0\n119\treturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n120\t```\n121\t\n122\tiii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n123\t\n124\t## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n125\t\n126\ti. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n127\t\n128\tii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n129\t```python\n130\tinside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n131\treturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n132\t```\n133\t```python\n134\tneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n135\t```\n136\t\n137\tiii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n138\t\n139\t## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n140\t\n141\ti. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n142\t\n143\tii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n144\t```python\n145\tFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n146\t```\n147\t```python\n148\t'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n149\t```\n150\t\n151\tiii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n152\t\n153\t## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* derived from?\n154\t\n155\ti. From `SoundFr`, the frame at which the sound cue was played in each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n156\t\n157\tii. The frame times of the session, and the cue of every trial placed on them:\n158\t```python\n159\tframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n160\tcue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n161\t```\n162\t\n163\tiii. The timing is dervied from the frame number.\n164\t\n165\t## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_to_sound_cue*?\n166\t\n167\ti. The cue frame is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the cue time minus the time of each bin, in seconds.  Note that here the instruction as *time_to_sound_cue*, so it's positive before the sound cue, and negative after the sound cue.\n168\t\n169\tii. The time axis of the trial, and the input built from it:\n170\t```python\n171\tframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n172\tcue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n173\t```\n174\t\n175\t```python\n176\ttime = frame_time[window]\n177\t```\n178\t```python\n179\tcue[trial] - time\n180\t```\n181\t\n182\tiii. The time need to be converted to seconds and compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n183\t\n184\t## 3-c. How is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* aligned with the neural data?\n185\t\n186\ti. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n187\t\n188\tii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n189\t```python\n190\ttime = frame_time[window]\n191\t```\n192\t\n193\tiii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n194\t\n195\t## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *day_of_training* derived from?\n196\t\n197\ti. From the session ids of a mouse, which carry the date and so put its sessions in order.\n198\t\n199\tii. The sessions of a mouse taken in order:\n200\t```python\n201\tfor subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n202\t```\n203\t\n204\t```python\n205\tdef training_days(records):\n206\t    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n207\t    days, count = {}, {}\n208\t\n209\t    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n210\t    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n211\t        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n212\t        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n213\t\n214\t    return days\n215\t```\n216\t\n217\tiii. The date string is the only field that orders every session of every mouse, so it is used to put them in order, and the *day_of_training* variable is computed from how many of the training session come before.\n218\t\n219\t## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *day_of_training*?\n220\t\n221\ti. The day is how many recorded days the mouse is into training, so its first session is 0 and each later one counts up, to at most 7. The count is over every session of the dataset, and is broadcast across the bins of a trial, however many it has.\n222\t\n223\tii. The count, and the broadcast:\n224\t```python\n225\tdef training_days(records):\n226\t    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n227\t    days, count = {}, {}\n228\t\n229\t    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n230\t    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n231\t        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n232\t        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n233\t\n234\t    return days\n235\t```\n236\t\n237\tiii. The recording days of a mouse are not consecutive, counting the days it was recorded on gives how far into training it is.\n238\t\n239\t## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_since_trial_start* derived from?\n240\t\n241\ti. From `StartFr`, the frame at which the mouse entered the corridor on each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n242\t\n243\tii. The corridor entry of every trial placed on the frame times:\n244\t```python\n245\tstart = np.interp(beh['StartFr'], index, frame_time)\n246\t```\n247\t\n248\tiii. N/A\n249\t\n250\t## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_since_trial_start*?\n251\t\n252\ti. The entry frame is fractional, so it is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the time of each bin minus that entry, in seconds, starting near zero. Note that this is *time_since_trial_start* so it is negative before start, postive after.\n253\t\n254\tii. The input built from the trial time axis:\n255\t```python\n256\ttime - start[trial]\n257\t```\n258\t\n259\tiii. Simply compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n260\t\n261\t## 5-c. How is `input` *time_since_trial_start* aligned with the neural data?\n262\t\n263\ti. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n264\t\n265\tii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n266\t```python\n267\ttime = frame_time[window]\n268\t```\n269\t\n270\tiii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n271\t\n272\t## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *reward_availability* derived from?\n273\t\n274\ti. From `isRew`, which marks the trials run in the rewarded corridor.\n275\t\n276\tii. The flag of every trial:\n277\t```python\n278\treward = beh['isRew'].astype(int)\n279\t```\n280\t\n281\tiii. N/A\n282\t\n283\t## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *reward_availability*?\n284\t\n285\ti. N/A\n286\t\n287\tii. N/A\n288\t\n289\tiii. No processing neeed, it is false for every trial of the unsupervised and naive mice, which ran the same corridors with no water available.\n290\t\n291\t## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *visual_stimulus* derived from?\n292\t\n293\ti. From `WallName`, which names the texture on the walls of the corridor of each trial.\n294\t\n295\tii. The name of every trial, mapped to its code:\n296\t```python\n297\tstimulus = np.array([STIMULUS.index(TEXTURE[name]) for name in beh['WallName']])\n298\t```\n299\t\n300\tiii. Note that `TrialStim` names the stimulus of each trial as well, but in the swap sessions it is masked.\n301\t\n302\t## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *visual_stimulus*?\n303\t\n304\ti. The 15 names that appear across the dataset are mapped to their base texture through a hard coded table, giving four categories, and the texture is stored as its index into `STIMULUS`. The value is per trial, so it is broadcast across all of that trial's bins.\n305\t\n306\tii. The map and the categories:\n307\t```python\n308\tTEXTURE = {'circle1': 'circle', 'circle2': 'circle', 'circle3': 'circle',\n309\t           'leaf1': 'leaf', 'leaf2': 'leaf', 'leaf3': 'leaf',\n310\t           'leaf1_swap1': 'leaf', 'leaf1_swap2': 'leaf',\n311\t           'rock1': 'rock', 'rock2': 'rock',\n312\t           'wood1': 'wood', 'wood2': 'wood', 'wood5': 'wood',\n313\t           'wood1_swap1': 'wood', 'wood1_swap2': 'wood'}\n314\t\n315\tSTIMULUS = ['circle', 'leaf', 'rock', 'wood']\n316\t```\n317\t```python\n318\tnp.full(n_bins, stimulus[trial])\n319\t```\n320\t\n321\tiii. We use the broad cateogry of the stimulus texture as the category label for the visual stimuli.\n322\t\n323\t## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *licking* derived from?\n324\t\n325\ti. From `LickFr`, the neural frame number of every lick in the session.\n326\t\n327\tii. The lick frames, turned into a flag per frame:\n328\t```python\n329\tlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\n330\tlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n331\t```\n332\t\n333\tiii. The licking is directly aviliable from `LickFr`.\n334\t\n335\t## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *licking*?\n336\t\n337\ti. A frame is 1 if at least one lick falls in it and 0 otherwise. The frame number of a lick is fractional, so it is truncated to the frame it lands in.\n338\t\n339\tii. The flag per frame, and the values of one trial:\n340\t```python\n341\tlicking = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=int)\n342\tlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\n343\tlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n344\t```\n345\t```python\n346\tLICKING = ['no lick', 'lick']\n347\tlicking[window]\n348\t```\n349\t\n350\tiii. Simply convert from the from lick_frame to a categorical variable.\n351\t\n352\t## 8-c. How is `output` *licking* aligned with the neural data?\n353\t\n354\ti. `LickFr` indexes the imaging frames, so the flag is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n355\t\n356\tii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n357\t```python\n358\tneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n359\tlicking[window]\n360\t```\n361\t\n362\tiii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n363\t\n364\t## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *position* derived from?\n365\t\n366\ti. From `ft_Pos`, the position inside the corridor at each imaging frame, in decimeters: 0 to 40 across the texture and on to 60 through the grey space.\n367\t\n368\tii. The position of every frame, binned:\n369\t```python\n370\tposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\n371\t```\n372\t\n373\tiii. Simply convert from decimeters to meters, and split into 4 categories.\n374\t\n375\t## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *position*?\n376\t\n377\ti. The position is divided by 10 decimeters, which gives the four 1 m bins the task asks for, and stored as an index into `POSITION`.\n378\t\n379\tii. The bins and the values of one trial:\n380\t```python\n381\tPOSITION = ['0-1 m', '1-2 m', '2-3 m', '3-4 m']\n382\t```\n383\t```python\n384\tposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\n385\tposition[window]\n386\t```\n387\t\n388\tiii. The frames of a trial are only those inside the texture, where the position stays below 40.\n389\t\n390\t## 9-d. How is `output` *position* aligned with the neural data?\n391\t\n392\ti. `ft_Pos` gives one position per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n393\t\n394\tii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n395\t```python\n396\tneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n397\tposition[window]\n398\t```\n399\t\n400\tiii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n401\t\n402\t## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *running_speed* derived from?\n403\t\n404\ti. From `ft_RunSpeed`, the running speed of the mouse at each imaging frame.\n405\t\n406\tii. The speed of the frames the dataset keeps:\n407\t```python\n408\tspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\n409\t```\n410\t\n411\tiii. Directly use running speed and discretize into categories.\n412\t\n413\t## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *running_speed*?\n414\t\n415\ti. The speeds are split into four bins holding a quarter of the frames each. The split is on rank rather than on a threshold, and is taken over the frames the dataset keeps, once per session, so the frames of a dropped trial do not move the boundaries.\n416\t\n417\tii. The split, and where it is applied:\n418\t```python\n419\tdef quartiles(values):\n420\t    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n421\t    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n422\t    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n423\t    return rank * 4 // values.size\n424\t```\n425\t```python\n426\tkept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\n427\tspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\n428\tspeed[window]\n429\t```\n430\t\n431\tiii. Up to a third of the frames of a session sit at exactly zero speed, so we take a rank ordering and divide into 4 equal bins.\n432\t\n433\t## 10-d. How is `output` *running_speed* aligned with the neural data?\n434\t\n435\ti. `ft_RunSpeed` gives one speed per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n436\t\n437\tii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n438\t```python\n439\tneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n440\tspeed[window]\n441\t```\n442\t\n443\tiii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n444\t\n445\t## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n446\t\n447\ti. The behavior can run past the imaging, so every stream is cut to the number of imaged frames, and a lick recorded after the last imaged frame is dropped. A trial left with no frames is dropped.\n448\t\n449\tii. The cut to the imaged frames, the licks kept, and the failure that does not stop the run:\n450\t```python\n451\tn_frames = spikes.shape[1]\n452\tframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n453\t```\n454\t```python\n455\tlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n456\t```\n457\t\n458\tiii. The reference code cuts the same way, `beh[...][:nfr]` with `nfr = spk.shape[1]`. Beyond that there is nothing to handle. This is a very clean dataset.\n459\t\n460\t## 12-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n\nii. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names, and `subject_idx` holds each session's index into that list: 89 sessions over 19 mice.\n\nii. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading the spike files, which total 405 GB.\n\nii. A behavior file is read once for the sessions it holds and released before the next one, and a session's traces are released when the session is done.\n\nii. bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    # a quarter or more of the frames can sit at exactly zero speed, and no threshold can\n    # cut a tie like that into equal parts, so the split is on rank rather than on value\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n\n\n# --- neural This suggests behavior, neural activity, and anatomical/area metadata are stored separately and linked by session id.\n66\t\n67\t### Dataset Size (from data files)\n68\t| Statistic | Value |\n69\t|-----------|-------|\n70\t| Neurons (total) | To be computed from `spks` lists during conversion |\n71\t| Neurons / session | Variable; per-session spike file contains `spks` list |\n72\t| Subjects | 19 (from spike filenames; behavior summary to reconcile) |\n73\t| Sessions / subject | Variable across mice |\n74\t| Trials (total) | 25287 |\n75\t| Trials / session | mean 352.2, min/max to verify from summary output |\n76\t\n77\t---\n78\t\n79\t## Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n80\t**Status**: COMPLETE\n81\t\n82\t### Expected Statistics (from paper/methods)\n83\t| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n84\t|-----------|-------|--------------|\n85\t| Neurons (total) | To be computed from `spks` lists during conversion | |\n86\t| Neurons / session | Variable; per-session spike file contains `spks` list | |\n87\t| Subjects | 19 (from spike filenames; behavior summary to reconcile) | |\n88\t| Sessions / subject | Variable across mice | |\n89\t| Trials (total) | 25287 | |\n90\t| Trials / session | mean 352.2, min/max to verify from summary output | |\n91\t| Neural data time bin | Not explicitly stated in methods excerpt | Analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces. |\n92\t| Behavior data time bin | | |\n93\t| Reward rate | Rewarded corridor only; unsupervised had no rewards | Reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor... rewards were absent in the unsupervised training experiment. |\n94\t| <Task/behavior statistic 1> | | |\n95\t| <Task/behavior statistic 2> | | |\n96\t| ... | | |\n97\t\n98\t\n99\t### Processing Details\n100\tImaging data were processed with Suite2p, including motion correction, ROI detection, cell classification, neuropil correction, and spike deconvolution. All analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces. The original task used 4 m virtual corridors with 2 m grey inter-corridor space. For imaging mice, sound cue position was uniformly random between 0.5 and 3.5 m. For behavior-only training, reward zone onset was uniformly random between 2 and 3 m and extended to corridor end. The paper states that only running timepoints were considered for analysis, removing periods when task mice stopped to collect rewards. For our conversion, trials must be aligned to corridor entry/trial start while preserving these event relationships.\n101\t\n102\t### Curation Steps\n103\t\n104\t**Neuron curation rules**:\n105\tNo explicit neuron exclusion thresholds found yet in methods excerpt beyond Suite2p cell classification / preprocessing; need to match available curated outputs in source data/reference code.\n106\t\n107\t**Trial curation rules**:\n108\tReference analyses considered only running timepoints, excluding stopped periods during reward collection. Need to determine from code/data whether entire trials are excluded or only non-running frames are masked.\n109\t\n110\t### Decoders Trained\n111\t| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n112\t| | `lickCount`, `lick_response` | Time-varying binary output |\n143\t| framewise position / accumulated position | output[2] position bin | Discretize 4 m corridor into 4 equal 1 m bins | `spk_pos_interp`, `get_interpPos_spk` | Time-varying categorical output |\n144\t| `Run` / `RunFr` | output[3] running speed bin | Discretize running speed into quartiles over valid data | running variables in behavior | Time-varying categorical output |\n145\t| retinotopy `iarea` | brain_region_idx | Map area ids to coarse region names via `neu_area_ID` | `load_retino`, `neu_area_ID` | Match retinotopy by normalized subject+date id; may need to repeat mapping across concatenated `spks` groups |\n146\t\n147\t### Key Decisions\n148\t1. **Use spike session files as the session backbone**: Paper reports 89 recordings in 19 mice and `/app/data/spk` contains exactly 89 imaging-session files.\n149\t2. **Treat `spks` as already processed neural activity**: Reference code loads `spks` directly and paper states analyses use deconvolved fluorescence traces, so no dF/F or deconvolution should be recomputed.\n150\t3. **Concatenate all `spks` list elements across neurons**: `load_spk` explicitly concatenates the list along axis 0, so all groups belong to one session.\n151\t4. **Align trials to corridor entry/trial start**: This is required by the decoder task, while preserving reference framewise relationships among cue, lick, position, and reward.\n152\t5. **Use only imaging sessions with both neural and behavior data**: Behavior contains extra entries such as `swap1`/`swap2` and behavior-only subjects that should not define neural sessions.\n153\t6. **Handle retinotopy by normalized subject+date id**: Retinotopy filenames omit run suffix, so they must be matched to spike sessions by subject and date rather than full session id.\n154\t7. **Respect running-only analysis where applicable**: Paper states only running timepoints were analyzed, so stationary reward-collection periods should be masked or excluded when constructing time-varying variables.\n155\t\n156\t### Planned Sanity Checks\n157\t- [ ] Check 1: For a representative session, matches raw behavior values.\n160\t- [ ] Check 4: Verify position-bin labels correspond to raw position ranges [0,1), [1,2), [2,3), [3,4] m.\n161\t- [ ] Check 5: Verify retinotopy-derived brain-region All analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces. The original task used 4 m virtual corridors with 2 m grey inter-corridor space. For imaging mice, sound cue position was uniformly random between 0.5 and 3.5 m. For behavior-only training, reward zone onset was uniformly random between 2 and 3 m and extended to corridor end. The paper states that only running timepoints were considered for analysis, removing periods when task mice stopped to collect rewards. For our conversion, trials must be aligned to corridor entry/trial start while preserving these event relationships.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\nNo explicit neuron exclusion thresholds found yet in methods excerpt beyond Suite2p cell classification / preprocessing; need to match available curated outputs in source data/reference code.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\nReference analyses considered only running timepoints, excluding stopped periods during reward collection. subject+date id; may need to repeat mapping across concatenated `spks` groups |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use spike session files as the session backbone**: Paper reports 89 recordings in 19 mice and `/app/data/spk` contains exactly 89 imaging-session files.\n2. **Treat `spks` as already processed neural activity**: Reference code loads `spks` directly and paper states analyses use deconvolved fluorescence traces, so no dF/F or deconvolution should be recomputed.\n3. **Concatenate all `spks` list elements across neurons**: `load_spk` explicitly concatenates the list along axis 0, so all groups belong to one session.\n4. **Align trials to corridor entry/trial start**: This is required by the decoder task, while preserving reference framewise relationships among cue, lick, position, and reward.\n5. **Use only imaging sessions with both neural and behavior data**: Behavior contains extra entries such as `swap1`/`swap2` and behavior-only subjects that should not define neural sessions.\n6. **Handle retinotopy by normalized subject+date id**: Retinotopy filenames omit run suffix, so they must be matched to spike sessions by subject and date rather than full session id.\n7. **Respect running-only analysis where applicable**: Paper states only running timepoints were analyzed, so stationary reward-collection periods should be masked or excluded when constructing time-varying variables.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Check 1: For a representative session, verify concatenated neural matrix shape equals sum of `spks[i].shape[0]` across list elements and How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI does not use the master index `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy`. Instead it (1) eagerly loads **every** `.npy` file in `/app/data/beh` into one flat dictionary keyed by the behavior entry key, and (2) takes the list of `spk/*_neural_data.npy` filenames as the authoritative list of imaging sessions (89 files). Retinotopy is loaded per session from `retinotopy/<mouse>_<Y>_<M>_<D>_trans.npz`, i.e. the session id with the block suffix stripped. Because the flat behavior dictionary is built by iterating files in sorted order and assigning `entries[sid] = sess`, a session that appears in more than one `Beh_*.npy` file is silently resolved to whichever file sorts **last**. `Imaging_Exp_info.npy` and `example_bef_and_aft_learning_behavior.npy` are also loaded into the same dict; their keys are experiment-type strings rather than session ids so they are never matched (harmless but wasteful). No behavior file is ever released, so all 25 behavior files stay resident for the whole run.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_all_behavior_entries():\n    entries = {}\n    source_file = {}\n    for f in sorted(BEH_ROOT.glob('*.npy')):\n        dat = np.load(f, allow_pickle=True).item()\n        for sid, sess in dat.items():\n            entries[sid] = sess\n            source_file[sid] = f.name\n    return entries, source_file\n\n\ndef choose_behavior_for_spike_session(spike_sid, beh_entries):\n    if spike_sid in beh_entries:\n        return spike_sid\n    candidates = [k for k in beh_entries if k == spike_sid or k.startswith(spike_sid + '_')]\n    if candidates:\n        for c in candidates:\n            if 'swap' not in c:\n                return c\n        return candidates[0]\n    return None\n\n\ndef load_spike_session(spike_sid):\n    obj = np.load(SPK_ROOT / f'{spike_sid}_neural_data.npy', allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spks = obj['spks']\n    return np.concatenate([x.astype(np.float32, copy=False) for x in spks], axis=0)\n\n\ndef load_retino_for_session(spike_sid):\n    rid = normalize_session_for_retino(spike_sid)\n    path = RET_ROOT / f'{rid}_trans.npz'\n    ...\n```\n```python\nspike_sessions = sorted(f.name.replace('_neural_data.npy', '') for f in SPK_ROOT.glob('*_neural_data.npy'))\nmatched = []\nfor sid in spike_sessions:\n    b = choose_behavior_for_spike_session(sid, beh_entries)\n    if b is not None:\n        matched.append((sid, b))\n```\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5, Key Decisions 1, 3, 5 and 6: *\"Use spike session files as the session backbone: Paper reports 89 recordings in 19 mice and `/app/data/spk` contains exactly 89 imaging-session files\"*; *\"Concatenate all `spks` list elements across neurons: `load_spk` explicitly concatenates the list along axis 0\"*; *\"Use only imaging sessions with both neural and behavior data: Behavior contains extra entries such as `swap1`/`swap2` and behavior-only subjects that should not define neural sessions\"*; *\"Handle retinotopy by normalized subject+date id: Retinotopy filenames omit run suffix\"*. Step 4 notes 89 spike files / 123 behavior entries / 89 retinotopy files and 19 subjects from the spike filenames, consistent with the paper.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. The subject id is the substring of the session id before the first underscore (the mouse name). Subjects are accumulated in first-encounter order over the sessions that survive session filtering, and `subject_idx` is the index of each session's mouse in that list. Because of the session filter (see 1-c/1-e) only 5 of the dataset's 19 mice reach the output: TX108, TX109, TX60, TX61, VR2.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_session_id(session_id):\n    parts = session_id.split('_')\n    subj = parts[0]\n    date = '_'.join(parts[1:4])\n    run = parts[4] if len(parts) > 4 else None\n    return subj, date, run\n```\n```python\nfor day_value, (sid, bkey) in enumerate(matched):\n    subj, date, run = parse_session_id(sid)\n    if subj not in subject_to_idx:\n        subject_to_idx[subj] = len(subjects)\n        subjects.append(subj)\n    ...\n    data['subject_idx'].append(subject_to_idx[subj])\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 4: *\"Session ids encode mouse ids ... Subject count consistent; use subject prefix before first underscore as subject id.\"* The AI verified 19 unique prefixes across the 89 spike files and matched this to the paper's \"19 mice\".\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. A session = one `spk/<mouse>_<date>_<blk>_neural_data.npy` file, i.e. one mouse / date / block, matching the reference's definition. No de-duplication is needed because the spike directory lists each recording once. However, the AI then **restricts the converted dataset to 27 of the 89 sessions**: a session is kept only if its behavior has `isRew.any() == True` **and** at least one entry in `LickFr`. This removes every unsupervised, naive and grating session (the paradigms the paper is actually about) and 14 of the 19 mice. Output: 27 sessions, 5 subjects, 11,444 trials (reference: 89 sessions, 19 subjects, 37,728 trials).\n\nii.\n```python\nrewarded = []\nfor sid, b in matched:\n    sess = beh_entries[b]\n    isrew = np.asarray(sess.get('isRew', []))\n    lickn = len(np.asarray(sess.get('LickFr', [])))\n    if isrew.size and bool(isrew.any()) and lickn > 0:\n        rewarded.append((sid, b))\nif args.sample:\n    matched = rewarded[:2] if len(rewarded) >= 2 else matched[:2]\nelse:\n    matched = rewarded\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10: *\"Included many unsupervised/naive/grating sessions in the first full conversion, causing 62/89 sessions to have zero lick fraction and 61/89 to have zero reward availability. **Resolution**: restricted full conversion to sessions with `isRew.any()` and nonzero `LickFr`.\"* The trajectory (step 48) shows the same reasoning: constant-zero lick and reward variables in the sample run were read as a bug in behavior mapping rather than as the true state of an unsupervised session. The AI also notes this reduced file size from ~42 GB to ~16 GB.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trial boundaries are **inferred heuristically** rather than read from the data. `infer_trial_frame_bounds` uses only `SoundFr`: `starts[1:] = SoundFr[1:] - max(diff(SoundFr), 1)`, which algebraically equals `SoundFr[:-1]`; `starts[0] = 0`; `ends[t] = starts[t+1] - 1`. So the window assigned to trial *t* is `[SoundFr[t-1], SoundFr[t])` — it begins at the **previous** trial's sound cue and ends at this trial's cue. A uniform equal-split fallback exists if `SoundFr` is absent (never triggered; all 27 sessions report `soundfr_heuristic`). All `ntrials` windows are emitted, and they tile the whole session including the 2 m grey inter-corridor space.\n\nThe behavior dictionaries do in fact contain explicit boundaries — `StartFr` (corridor entry), `GrayFr`, `EndFr`, plus per-frame `ft_trInd` and `ft_CorrSpc` — which the reference uses. Measured on `TX60_2021_06_07_1` the heuristic starts differ from `StartFr` by a mean of **32 frames**, which is about one whole trial (median traversal ≈ 23 frames).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef infer_trial_frame_bounds(sess, n_frames):\n    ntrials = int(sess['ntrials'])\n    candidates = [k for k in sess.keys() if 'tr' in k.lower() and hasattr(sess[k], 'shape')]\n    sound_fr = np.asarray(sess['SoundFr']).astype(int) if 'SoundFr' in sess else None\n    if sound_fr is not None and sound_fr.shape[0] == ntrials:\n        starts = np.zeros(ntrials, dtype=int)\n        starts[1:] = np.maximum(sound_fr[1:] - np.maximum(np.diff(sound_fr), 1), 0)\n        ends = np.empty(ntrials, dtype=int)\n        ends[:-1] = np.maximum(starts[1:] - 1, starts[:-1])\n        ends[-1] = n_frames - 1\n        return starts, ends, {'method': 'soundfr_heuristic', 'candidates': candidates}\n    edges = np.linspace(0, n_frames, ntrials + 1).astype(int)\n    return edges[:-1], edges[1:] - 1, {'method': 'uniform_fallback', 'candidates': candidates}\n```\n```python\nfor tr in range(ntrials):\n    s = int(max(0, starts[tr]))\n    e = int(min(n_frames - 1, max(starts[tr], ends[tr])))\n    trial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 6: *\"Current implementation uses heuristic trial boundary inference and placeholder time-bin assumptions pending validation.\"* Step 10: *\"trial boundaries remain heuristic (`soundfr_heuristic`). **Resolution**: documented as remaining caveats requiring justification/possible future refinement.\"* Step 12 attributes the near-chance position decoding to this: *\"consistent with the current conversion using heuristic trial boundaries (`soundfr_heuristic`) rather than explicit corridor-entry frame annotations.\"* The trajectory shows why: the one script the agent wrote to dump the behavior keys (step 48) crashed on a `np.nanmin` type error before printing `all keys sample`, and was never retried — the strings `StartFr`, `EndFr`, `ft_trInd`, `ft_CorrSpc` never appear anywhere in the 293-step trajectory.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. **No trial-level quality control is applied at all.** Every one of a session's `ntrials` windows is written out. Windows are only clamped to the array bounds (`s = max(0, starts[tr])`, `e = min(n_frames-1, ...)`). In particular there is no drop of trials that were not imaged, and no length outlier rejection: the converted data contains trials of up to 593 time bins (≈ 2,965 imaging frames, ~15 min), which are animals that stopped rather than traversals. The only curation is at the *session* level (see 1-c): 62 of 89 sessions are discarded.\n\nii.\n```python\n    for tr in range(ntrials):\n        s = int(max(0, starts[tr]))\n        e = int(min(n_frames - 1, max(starts[tr], ends[tr])))\n        ...\n        neural_trials.append(trial_spk)\n        input_trials.append(inp)\n        output_trials.append(out)\n```\n(no `continue`, no length test, no emptiness test anywhere in the trial loop)\n\niii. No justification is given for the absence of trial filtering; CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 3 does record the relevant paper statement — *\"The paper states that only running timepoints were considered for analysis, removing periods when task mice stopped to collect rewards\"* — and Step 5 Key Decision 7 promises *\"Respect running-only analysis where applicable ... stationary reward-collection periods should be masked or excluded\"*, but this was never implemented. The session-level filter is justified in Step 10 as removing \"degenerate\" outputs (see 1-c).\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy` — a list of per-imaging-plane `(n_neurons, n_frames)` arrays — concatenated along the neuron axis, exactly as the reference `load_spk` does. The per-neuron visual area comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_spike_session(spike_sid):\n    obj = np.load(SPK_ROOT / f'{spike_sid}_neural_data.npy', allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spks = obj['spks']\n    return np.concatenate([x.astype(np.float32, copy=False) for x in spks], axis=0)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 Key Decision 3: *\"`load_spk` explicitly concatenates the list along axis 0, so all groups belong to one session.\"* Step 4: *\"Spike files already contain processed `spks`; ... Treat `spks` as already processed deconvolved activity.\"*\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No dF/F, no deconvolution, no normalisation, no smoothing — the deconvolved traces are used as-is. The traces are up-cast to `float32` at load (an unnecessary copy of a ~1–3 GB array), sliced to the trial window, decimated by taking every 5th frame, and stored as `float16`. Trials keep whatever length their window has; nothing is padded.\n\nii.\n```python\nreturn np.concatenate([x.astype(np.float32, copy=False) for x in spks], axis=0)\n```\n```python\ntrial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 4/Step 5 Key Decision 2: *\"Reference code loads `spks` directly and paper states analyses use deconvolved fluorescence traces, so no dF/F or deconvolution should be recomputed.\"* For `float16`, Step 10: *\"Full dataset was too large in the initial dense representation (~42G ...). **Resolution**: stride-5 temporal downsampling and float16 neural storage reduced the filtered full dataset to ~16G.\"*\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. **No neurons are filtered.** Every neuron in `spks` is kept. `iarea` is mapped to a 5-element region list `['All', 'V1', 'medial', 'anterior', 'lateral']` with the index initialised to 0 (`'All'`), so any neuron whose `iarea` is not in `{1..8}` — i.e. `iarea == -1` (no area), `0`, and `9` — silently lands in a catch-all `'All'` bucket and is retained (411,493 neurons in the output). The mapping itself is also a guess: `V1 = (iarea == 7) | (iarea == 8)` and `medial = (iarea == 1) | (iarea == 2)`, whereas the reference has `V1 = [8]` and `mHV = [0, 1, 2, 9]`. Only `lHV = 5|6` and `aHV = 3|4`, the two lines of `neu_area_ID` the agent actually saw on screen, are right. If `len(iarea) != n_neurons` the index array is silently `np.resize`d (tiled or truncated) instead of raising.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef area_names_and_idx(iarea):\n    area_names = ['All', 'V1', 'medial', 'anterior', 'lateral']\n    iarea = np.asarray(iarea)\n    idx = np.zeros((len(iarea),), dtype=np.int64)\n    idx[(iarea == 7) | (iarea == 8)] = 1\n    idx[(iarea == 1) | (iarea == 2)] = 2\n    idx[(iarea == 3) | (iarea == 4)] = 3\n    idx[(iarea == 5) | (iarea == 6)] = 4\n    return area_names, idx\n```\n```python\nret = load_retino_for_session(sid)\nif ret is not None and 'iarea' in ret:\n    _, bri = area_names_and_idx(ret['iarea'])\n    if len(bri) != spk.shape[0]:\n        bri = np.resize(bri, spk.shape[0])\nelse:\n    bri = np.zeros((spk.shape[0],), dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 3: *\"No explicit neuron exclusion thresholds found yet in methods excerpt beyond Suite2p cell classification / preprocessing\"*, and Step 5 Key Decision 6 / the mapping table: *\"Map area ids to coarse region names via `neu_area_ID`\"*, *\"Match retinotopy by normalized subject+date id; may need to repeat mapping across concatenated `spks` groups\"*. The `areasN = ['All','V1','medial','anterior','lateral']` list was copied verbatim from the reference's `load_retino`, but in the reference `'All'` is the name of the *all-neurons* group in a plotting loop, not a brain region.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The metadata declares `'temporal_alignment_event': 'trial start / corridor entry'` and `off_start = 0.0`, `off_end = None`, and the README says *\"Trials are aligned to trial start / corridor entry\"*. In the code the window actually starts at the **previous trial's sound cue** (see 1-d), so on `TX60_2021_06_07_1` the true corridor entry sits on average 32 frames *after* the declared t = 0, i.e. roughly a whole trial late. Each trial's window is variable-length and unpadded, and inputs/outputs are sliced with the identical `s:e+1:frame_stride` slice, so the streams are internally consistent with each other even though they are collectively misaligned to the stated event.\n\nii.\n```python\n'temporal_alignment_event': 'trial start / corridor entry',\n'off_start': 0.0,\n'off_end': None,\n```\n```python\ns = int(max(0, starts[tr]))\ne = int(min(n_frames - 1, max(starts[tr], ends[tr])))\ntrial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 Key Decision 4: *\"Align trials to corridor entry/trial start: This is required by the decoder task.\"* Step 12 concedes the result: *\"position_bin is only marginally above chance ... consistent with the current conversion using heuristic trial boundaries (`soundfr_heuristic`) rather than explicit corridor-entry frame annotations.\"*\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Every stream is decimated by keeping every 5th imaging frame (`[:, ::frame_stride]`, `frame_stride = 5`), applied identically to neural, input and output arrays. This is **decimation, not rebinning** — the 4 frames between kept samples are thrown away rather than summed or averaged, so ~80% of the deconvolved spike mass is discarded. `metadata['time_bin_size']` is set to `5.0`; the format spec says this field is in **milliseconds**, but 5 frames at the 3.17 Hz imaging rate is ≈ **1,577 ms**, so the metadata is wrong by a factor of ~315. The AI never determined the imaging frame rate at all (Step 3: *\"Neural data time bin | Not explicitly stated in methods excerpt\"*). After decimation, mean trial length is 11.2 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_trial_matrices(spk, sess, day_value, stim_cats_global, frame_stride=5):\n    ...\n    trial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\n    T = trial_spk.shape[1]\n    ts = (np.arange(T, dtype=np.float32) * frame_stride)\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size': 5.0,\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7: *\"Temporal downsampling by stride 5 and float16 neural storage reduced sample size from ~12G to ~1.4G while preserving valid format.\"* Trajectory step 116: *\"this already suggests train_decoder is agnostic to exact time resolution as long as the dataset format is valid ... patch convert_data.py to add a temporal downsampling stride (e.g. 5 frames) ... Update metadata time_bin_size to reflect the stride in frame units.\"* So the field was knowingly written in frame units rather than the milliseconds the spec asks for.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr` (the imaging-frame number of the cue in each trial) and the trial window start `s`. Unlike the reference it does **not** use `ft` (the per-frame MATLAB timestamps), so the result is in frames, not seconds.\n\nii.\n```python\nsound_fr = np.asarray(sess.get('SoundFr', np.full(ntrials, -1)), dtype=int)\n...\ncue = sound_fr[tr] - s if tr < len(sound_fr) else -1\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 mapping table: *\"`SoundFr` or `SoundPos` with framewise time base → input[0] time to sound cue ... Decoder input requires continuous time to cue; derive from alignment and cue frame/position\"*, referencing the reference function `spk_2_cue`, which also indexes by `SoundFr`.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. `cue = SoundFr[tr] - s` converts the cue to a frame offset within the window; `ts = arange(T) * 5` is the frame offset of each retained bin; the input is `cue - ts`, i.e. **frames** remaining until the cue, positive before the cue and negative after (the same sign convention as the reference). `SoundFr` is truncated with `astype(int)` rather than interpolated. No conversion to seconds is done. Because the window is a previous-cue-to-cue span (1-d), the values run from roughly +one-trial-length down to 0 at the window end, and the full-data range is [-556, 2963] frames (≈ [-175 s, +934 s]) versus the reference's [-72.2 s, +73.4 s].\n\nii.\n```python\nts = (np.arange(T, dtype=np.float32) * frame_stride)\ncue = sound_fr[tr] - s if tr < len(sound_fr) else -1\ntime_to_cue = (cue - ts).astype(np.float32)\ninp = np.vstack([\n    time_to_cue,\n    np.full(T, float(day_value), dtype=np.float32),\n    ts.astype(np.float32),\n    np.full(T, float(reward_avail[tr]), dtype=np.float32),\n])\n```\n\niii. No explicit justification for the unit choice appears in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. Step 7 records the symptom without diagnosing it: *\"time_to_sound_cue range | [-557, 909]\"*, and Step 7's plot review notes *\"time_to_sound_cue ranges are very large, suggesting trial segmentation may still be off\"* — flagged but never acted on.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is built from `T = trial_spk.shape[1]`, the number of bins actually kept in the neural array, with the same `s` origin and the same stride-5 grid, so it is bin-for-bin aligned with the neural matrix of that trial. The alignment of the pair to the real corridor entry is the problem described in 2-d, not the alignment between the two streams.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\nT = trial_spk.shape[1]\nts = (np.arange(T, dtype=np.float32) * frame_stride)\ntime_to_cue = (cue - ts).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Implicit: all streams are derived from the same `(s, e, frame_stride)` triple, so they share one grid. The AI's Step 5 planned sanity check 3 (*\"Verify cue timing reconstructed from `SoundFr` aligns with trial bins and matches raw behavior values\"*) was never executed.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. From no raw variable. It is the position of the session in the `matched` list, produced by `enumerate`. Since `matched` derives from `sorted(spike_sessions)`, this is a running index over the whole converted dataset — 0, 1, 2, … 26 — that resets for no one. It therefore mixes \"which mouse\" with \"how far into training\": TX108's six sessions get 0–5, TX109's four get 6–9, and so on, so day-of-training and subject identity are perfectly confounded, and the input is a unique constant per session.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor day_value, (sid, bkey) in enumerate(matched):\n    subj, date, run = parse_session_id(sid)\n    ...\n    neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials, bounds_info, stim_names_local = \\\n        build_trial_matrices(spk, sess, day_value, all_stim_names, frame_stride=5)\n```\n\niii. Neither `StartFr` nor `ft` is read.\n\nii.\n```python\nts = (np.arange(T, dtype=np.float32) * frame_stride)\n```\n\niii. Implicit from the shared `(s, e, frame_stride)` slice.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Reward availability* derived from?\n\ni. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Reward availability*?\n\ni. Boolean → int, then `np.full(T, ...)` to broadcast the per-trial value across every bin of the trial. Range in the output is [0, 1] as expected; because only rewarded-paradigm sessions survive the session filter, both classes are present within each retained session.\n\nii.\n```python\nnp.full(T, float(reward_avail[tr]), dtype=np.float32),\n```\n\niii. From `TrialStim` when present, falling back to `WallName`. `TrialStim` is present in every session, so `WallName` is never used. The reference deliberately uses `WallName` instead, because `TrialStim` is masked in the swap sessions. That matters here: three of the AI's 27 sessions are matched to a `swap` behavior entry (`TX108_2023_04_07_1_swap2`, `TX61_2021_06_23_1_swap1`, `TX61_2021_06_25_1_swap2`, plus `VR2_2021_05_04_1_swap1` and `VR2_2021_05_06_1_swap2`), and for e.g. `TX108_2023_04_07_1_swap2` `TrialStim` reports `{circle1, leaf1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2}` while `WallName` — the texture actually on the walls — reports `{rock1, wood1, wood1_swap2, wood2}`. Those trials carry the wrong stimulus label.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef get_stimulus_categories(sess):\n    if 'TrialStim' in sess:\n        vals = np.asarray(sess['TrialStim']).astype(str)\n        cats = sorted(np.unique(vals).tolist())\n        mapping = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(cats)}\n        return vals, cats, np.array([mapping[v] for v in vals], dtype=np.int64)\n    if 'WallName' in sess:\n        vals = np.asarray(sess['WallName']).astype(str)\n        ...\n    raise KeyError('No TrialStim or WallName in behavior session')\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 mapping table: *\"`TrialStim` and/or `WallName` → output[0] visual stimulus category | Map strings to categorical labels per trial | `get_cat_id` | Categories include naturalistic stimuli such as circle, leaf, rock, brick and test variants.\"* No reason is given for preferring `TrialStim`, and the swap-key caveat is only noted in the abstract: Step 10, *\"Some sessions are matched to `swap1`/`swap2` behavior entries ... documented as remaining caveats.\"*\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. A **global vocabulary** is built by taking the sorted union of the raw stimulus strings over all retained sessions, and each trial's raw string is mapped to its index in that vocabulary. No grouping into base textures is done, so the classes are `circle1, circle2, leaf1, leaf1_swap1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2, leaf3` — 7 classes that split a single texture family (leaf) five ways, rather than the 4 texture categories (`circle, leaf, rock, wood`) the task asks for. The per-trial label is broadcast across all bins of the trial. Most sessions contain only 2–3 of the 7 classes, so the global chance level reported by the decoder (1/7 = 0.143) understates the real difficulty. Building the vocabulary requires a second pass over `get_stimulus_categories` for every session.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_stim_names = set()\nfor _, b in matched:\n    sess = beh_entries[b]\n    vals, cats, ids = get_stimulus_categories(sess)\n    all_stim_names.update(cats)\nall_stim_names = sorted(all_stim_names)\n```\n```python\nstim_vals, stim_names_local, stim_ids = get_stimulus_categories(sess)\nstim_map_global = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(stim_cats_global)}\nstim_ids_global = np.array([stim_map_global[v] for v in stim_vals], dtype=np.int64)\n...\nnp.full(T, stim_ids_global[tr], dtype=np.int64),\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10 lists *\"global visual-category vocabulary spanning multiple stimulus sets\"* among the remaining concerns; Step 11 notes *\"Above chance (0.1429); multiple stimulus categories across sessions.\"* No argument is made for keeping crop-level names rather than the four base textures the decoder spec names.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Licking* derived from?\n\ni. From `LickFr` (the imaging-frame number of each lick) together with `LickTrind` (the trial each lick belongs to). The reference uses `LickFr` alone.\n\nii.\n```python\nlick_fr = np.asarray(sess.get('LickFr', []), dtype=int)\nlick_tr = np.asarray(sess.get('LickTrind', []), dtype=int)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 mapping table: *\"`LickFr`, `LickTrind` → output[1] licking | Convert to binary time series per trial/bin | `spk_2_cue`, `spk_2_firstLick`, `lickCount`, `lick_response` | Time-varying binary output.\"*\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. For each trial the licks whose `LickTrind` equals that trial index are selected, their frame numbers are converted to a bin index by `(LickFr - s) // frame_stride`, any index outside `[0, T)` is dropped, and the corresponding bins are set to 1 (others 0). Because a bin spans 5 frames, one or more licks anywhere in those 5 frames marks the bin. Defensive clipping `idx = idx[idx < lick_fr.shape[0]]` was added after a session crashed with `LickTrind` longer than `LickFr`.\n\nii.\n```python\nlick = np.zeros(T, dtype=np.int64)\nif lick_tr.size and lick_fr.size:\n    idx = np.where(lick_tr == tr)[0]\n    idx = idx[idx < lick_fr.shape[0]]\n    if idx.size:\n        lf = ((lick_fr[idx] - s) // frame_stride).astype(int)\n        lf = lf[(lf >= 0) & (lf < T)]\n        if lf.size:\n            lick[lf] = 1\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10: *\"Full conversion crashed on a session with inconsistent lick arrays (`lick_tr` longer than `lick_fr`). **Resolution**: made lick indexing robust by clipping indices to valid `lick_fr` range and skipping empty arrays.\"*\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is written onto the same `T`-bin stride-5 grid as the neural array, so the array shapes and the time base agree. But the trial-membership gate (`LickTrind == tr`) and the window (`[SoundFr[tr-1], SoundFr[tr])`) disagree with each other: a lick belonging to trial *t* that occurs **after** trial *t*'s cue falls outside window *t*, and window *t+1* only accepts licks labelled *t+1*, so it is silently discarded. Since licking in the rewarded corridor is concentrated after the cue, this loses most licks — measured directly on `TX60_2021_06_07_1`, only **1,140 of 3,778 lick events (30%)** survive into the converted data.\n\nii.\n```python\nlf = ((lick_fr[idx] - s) // frame_stride).astype(int)\nlf = lf[(lf >= 0) & (lf < T)]\n```\n(`s` and `T` come from the same window/stride as `trial_spk = spk[:, s:e+1][:, ::frame_stride]`)\n\niii. No justification; the interaction was never noticed. The AI's Step 5 planned sanity check 2 — *\"For 3 spot-check trials, verify lick bins reconstructed from `LickFr`/`LickTrind` match raw lick events at the expected frame indices\"* — is listed as planned but no such per-lick check appears in the trajectory or in Step 10; the only lick check performed was whether a session had *any* lick events.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Position in corridor* derived from?\n\ni. **From nothing.** The code probes for a per-frame position array under the names `Pos`, `pos`, `AccumPos`, `AccPos`, `LickPos`. None of these is the real key — the behavior dictionaries store position as `ft_Pos` (per imaging frame, in decimetres) and `VRpos`/`VRposCum` (on the VR clock); `LickPos` exists but is per-lick, so its length never equals `n_frames`. The probe therefore always fails and the fallback fires: **position is fabricated as `np.linspace(0, 4, n_frames)`**, a linear ramp across the entire session. The converted \"position\" is thus a monotone function of absolute frame index in the session, not of the animal's location; within a ~40-frame trial it varies by only ~0.01 m, so it is effectively constant per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\npos = None\nfor k in ['Pos', 'pos', 'AccumPos', 'AccPos', 'LickPos']:\n    if k in sess and hasattr(sess[k], 'shape') and np.asarray(sess[k]).ndim == 1 and len(sess[k]) == n_frames:\n        pos = np.asarray(sess[k], dtype=float)\n        break\nif pos is None:\n    pos = np.linspace(0, 4, n_frames)\n```\n\niii. The fallback was written knowingly and never checked. Trajectory step 43: *\"position reconstruction may fall back to a synthetic linear ramp if no framewise position key matches ... Before Step 6 can be considered complete, we should at least run the script in sample mode to see whether it executes and learn what behavior keys actually exist for alignment.\"* The behavior-key dump that would have revealed `ft_Pos` (step 48) crashed on a `np.nanmin` type error before reaching its `print('all keys sample', ...)` line and was never re-run; `ft_Pos` appears nowhere in the 293-step trajectory. The Step 5 planned sanity check 4 — *\"Verify position-bin labels correspond to raw position ranges [0,1), [1,2), [2,3), [3,4] m\"* — was never executed.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. The synthetic ramp is floored into 1 m bins (see 9-c) once per session over all frames, then sliced per trial. No use of `ft_CorrSpc` to restrict to the textured corridor, and no handling of the 2 m grey space (which the ramp does not represent at all).\n\nii.\n```python\npos_bin = np.clip(np.floor(np.minimum(pos, 3.999999) / 1.0).astype(int), 0, 3)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5 mapping table: *\"framewise position / accumulated position → output[2] position bin | Discretize 4 m corridor into 4 equal 1 m bins | `spk_pos_interp`, `get_interpPos_spk` | Time-varying categorical output.\"*\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Four fixed 1 m bins, `floor(min(pos, 3.999999) / 1.0)` clipped to `[0, 3]`, labelled `bin0_0to1m … bin3_3to4m` — which is the thresholding rule the task asks for, but applied to fabricated values. This is not the variable the reference uses (`ft_RunSpeed`) but it is nearly a scalar multiple of it — measured correlation 0.9997 on `TX60_2021_06_07_1` — so as a source for a rank/quantile discretisation it is a legitimate substitute. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Once per session, the 25/50/75th percentiles of the per-frame running values are computed over **all** frames of the session — including the grey inter-corridor space and any frames not in any emitted trial — and `np.digitize` assigns each frame to a bin. The result is sliced per trial. The reference instead computes a rank-based split restricted to the frames the dataset actually keeps.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef quartile_bins(x):\n    x = np.asarray(x, dtype=float)\n    qs = np.nanquantile(x[np.isfinite(x)], [0.25, 0.5, 0.75])\n    return np.digitize(x, qs, right=False)\n```\n\niii. CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5: *\"Discretize running speed into quartiles over valid data.\"* No note explains why the quantiles are taken over the whole session rather than over the retained frames.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Four quantile bins via `np.digitize(x, [q25, q50, q75], right=False)`, labelled `q1…q4`. This breaks on the large tie at zero speed: roughly a quarter to a third of frames are exactly 0, so `q25 == 0`, and `np.digitize(0.0, [0, ...], right=False)` returns 1, dumping every stationary frame into `q2` and leaving `q1` **empty**. `verification_full_out.txt` confirms the failure in 9 of the 27 sessions — e.g. sessions with distribution `(0.000, 0.490, 0.250, 0.260)` and two sessions with `(0.000, 0.000, 0.732, 0.268)`. The Decoder Task requires *\"4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\"*; the reference achieves 0.2500/0.2500/0.2500/0.2500 by ranking with `argsort` instead of thresholding, precisely to break ties. The AI's global distribution is (0.144, 0.313, 0.288, 0.255).\n\nii.\n```python\nqs = np.nanquantile(x[np.isfinite(x)], [0.25, 0.5, 0.75])\nreturn np.digitize(x, qs, right=False)\n```\n```python\n'output_values': [ ..., ['q1', 'q2', 'q3', 'q4'] ]\n```\n\niii. The AI spotted the symptom twice and chose not to fix it.
